for further discussion). We do not make this remark anymore when we treat the probe magnitude as a generating function variable that can be used for differentiation.

Thm.
4.2.1
shows that the operators corresponding to the
m^{\prime}(a,b,c)=|\vec{\beta}|^{2a}\alpha_{1}^{b}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{c}
are observable when the measurement statistics are known for all
\gamma
in a neighborhood of
0
. Recall that at the beginning of Chap.
2
we made a brief note that the family of states we consider in this thesis are the states with rapidly decaying Wigner functions. This restriction on the set of states was necessary to ensure that the expectations of all polynomials of mode operators are defined. We also mentioned that Ref.
[
19
]
shows that all rapidly decaying Wigner functions are Schwartz functions, so this family of states is quite large and includes the important class of Gaussian states
[
40
]
. For the discussion of this section, this restriction means that the expectations of all operators corresponding to the
m^{\prime}(a,b,c)
are finite for
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
. The question is if these expectations determine
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
? At least for the set of two-mode twirled states with Husimi functions that have determinate complex moment sequences, the answer is affirmative. See Sec.
2.6
for a refresher on the relevant concepts.

The first thing to note is that since
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is in vacuum for the modes
\geq 3
, the operator corresponding to
m^{\prime}(k,j,i)
can be replaced by
(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{i}a_{1}^{j}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{k}a_{2}^{k}
as the expectations of all the other monomials after expanding the sum
\sum_{l=2}^{S}a_{l}^{\dagger}a_{l}
, and normally ordering the summands, vanish. Further, notice that we can use the commutation relations to express any anti-normally ordered monomial of the form
a_{1}^{i}(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{j}a_{2}^{k}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{k}
as a (finite) linear combination of the normally-ordered monomials
(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{i}a_{1}^{j}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{k}a_{2}^{k}
. This is because the application of the commutation relation for a given mode does not change the difference in the powers of the creation and annihilation operators for that mode. Therefore, the expectations of the
a_{1}^{i}(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{j}a_{2}^{k}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{k}
are also determined by the measurement statistics under the assumptions of Thm.
4.2.1
. We note that, for the rest of this section, by a “linear combination” we mean a finite linear combination.

Corollary 4.2.2
.

Consider a WFH configuration characterized by a BS with a trivial partition and with one photon counter. Assume the measurement statistics are known for all
\gamma
in a neighborhood of
0
. Denote the Husimi function of
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
by
Q(\vec{\delta},\vec{\delta}^{*})
, where
\vec{\delta}=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
. If the complex moment sequence of
Q(\vec{\delta},\vec{\delta}^{*})
is determinate, then
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is determined by the measurement statistics.

Proof.

As discussed in the paragraphs before the corollary statement, the assumptions of the statement about the available measurement statistics imply, according to Thm.
4.2.1
and due to the fact that
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
occupies the first two modes, that the expectations of all operators of the form
a_{1}^{i}(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{j}a_{2}^{k}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{k}
are determined. Further, the expectations of the anti-normally operators not of this form are zero. To see this, notice that every operator in this set is of the form
a_{1}^{i}(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{j}a_{2}^{k}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{l}
, where
k\neq l
. Since
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is of the form
\tilde{\rho}_{2}=\sum_{i}\chi_{i}\otimes\ket{i}\bra{i}
, first taking the partial trace of its product with
a_{1}^{i}(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{j}a_{2}^{k}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{l}
over mode
1
leaves us with a (possibly infinite) sum of expectations of
a_{2}^{k}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{l}
, each with a Fock state in mode
2
. The latter all vanish since
a_{2}^{k}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{l}
takes a Fock state to a different Fock state when
k\neq l
.

We now use the correspondence between the expectations of anti-normally ordered monomials of mode operators and the moments of the Husimi function. For
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
, this correspondence is given by

\displaystyle\tr(\tilde\rho_2 a_1^i (a_1^\dagger)^j a_2^k (a_2^\dagger)^l)=\int d\vec{\delta}d\vec{\delta}^{*}Q(\vec{\delta},\vec{\delta}^{*})\delta_{1}^{i}(\delta_{1}^{*})^{j}\delta_{2}^{k}(\delta_{2}^{*})^{l}

(4.2.5)

for all non-negative integers
i,j,k,l
. The set of integrals on the right-hand side of the identity comprise the set of complex moments of
Q(\vec{\delta},\vec{\delta}^{*})
. By the preceding discussion, these are all determined by the form of
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
and by the measurement statistics. And, since, by the assumption of the corollary statement, the complex moment sequence of
Q(\vec{\delta},\vec{\delta}^{*})
is determinate,
Q(\vec{\delta},\vec{\delta}^{*})
is determined by the measurement statistics. Therefore,
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is determined as well.
∎

Analytic arguments can be used to show that the expectations of the
operators associated with polynomials in
|\vec{\beta}|^{2}
,

\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{1}^{*}
can be determined given the counter
probability distributions for
\gamma
in any neighborhood, not just
neighborhoods of
0
. But practical concerns imply that these
expectations be determined from a finite set of
\gamma
only. In
this case, it is likely not possible to determine the desired
expectations unconditionally. Instead, we consider expectations for
states with less than
N
photons in the modes. With this bound on
photon number, because of normal ordering, operators associated with
monomials in
\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}^{*}
, where either the total degree of the creation operators or the total degree of the annihilation operators in the monomial is greater than
N
, are guaranteed to have zero expectation. It
therefore suffices to construct the
m^{\prime}(a,b,c)=|\vec{\beta}|^{2a}\alpha_{1}^{b}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{c}
in the set
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\{m^{\prime}(a,b,c):a+\max(b,c)\leq N\}
. A stronger result
is the next theorem, for which we need the following
definitions. Let
\mathcal{P}_{N}
be the vector space spanned by the monomials
\gamma^{k}(\gamma^{*})^{l}
for
\gamma\in\mathbb{C}
with
k\leq N
,
l\leq N
. Let

\mathcal{M}=\{m(a,b,c):a+\max(b,c)\leq N\}
, where
m(a,b,c)=|\vec{\alpha}|^{2a}\alpha_{1}^{b}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{c}
. Let us further introduce
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}=\{m^{\prime}(a,b,c):b\leq c,a+c\leq N\}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}_{>}=\{m(a,b,c):b\leq c,a+c\leq N\}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
for future results. Note the implicit dependence of
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{M}_{>}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}
on
N
.

Theorem 4.2.3
.

Let a finite set
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{C}
satisfy the following
condition: For all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{N}
such that
p(\Gamma)=\{0\}
, we
have
p=0
. Let the input state have maximum
N
photons. Then, given the counter outcome distributions for all
elements
\gamma\in\Gamma
, the members of
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
are
observable.

Proof.

Eq.
4.2.4
shows that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is in the span
of
\mathcal{M}
, so it suffices to prove the statement for
\mathcal{M}
. To see the former, note that for non-negative integers
a,b,c
the corresponding
m^{\prime}(a,b,c)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
m(a,b,c)\in\mathcal{M}
iff
a+\max(b,c)\leq N
, and then notice that the
m(j,b+a-j,c+a-j)
appearing in the expansion of
m^{\prime}(a,b,c)
in Eq.
4.2.4
satisfy this property - that is,
j+\max(b+a-j,c+a-j)=j+\max(b,c)+a-j=a+\max(b,c)
. The
assumption on
\Gamma
in the theorem statement implies that the map
p\in\mathcal{P}_{N}\mapsto(p(\gamma,\gamma^{*}))_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
is linear and
injective. It follows that for any given monomial
p=\gamma^{l}(\gamma^{*})^{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{N}
, there is a sequence
(c_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
such that for all
monomials
q\in\mathcal{P}_{N}
different from
p
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}q(\gamma,\gamma^{*})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}p(\gamma,\gamma^{*})

\displaystyle=1.

(4.2.6)

We can sum the generating function
F_{1}=\exp((|\vec{\alpha}|^{2}+ \alpha_1\gamma^*+
\alpha_1^*\gamma)u)
for values of
\gamma\in\Gamma

accordingly, and define
F_{1,p}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}F_{1}(u,\gamma)
.
For
n\leq 2N
, the coefficient of
u^{n}
of
F_{1,p}
is given by

\displaystyle(n!)\mathrm{coeff}_{u^{n}}F_{1,p}

\displaystyle=\left[\partial_{u}^{n}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}F_{1}(u,\gamma)\right]_{u=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}\left[\partial_{u}^{n}F_{1}(u,\gamma)\right]_{u=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}(|\vec{\alpha}|^{2}+\alpha_{1}\gamma^{*}+\alpha_{1}^{*}\gamma)^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i+j+m=n}\binom{n}{m,i,j}\alpha_{1}^{i}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{j}|\vec{\alpha}|^{2m}(\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}(\gamma^{*})^{i}\gamma^{j}).

(4.2.7)

If
n<k+l
,
n\leq N
, the whole expression vanishes because
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}(\gamma^{*})^{i}\gamma^{j}
vanishes for all values of
i
and
j
in the sum. For
n\geq k+l
the expectations of the operators associated with the summands on the right-hand side vanish for either
i+m>N
or
j+m>N
(see the discussion preceding the theorem statement). Therefore, the value of
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}(\gamma^{*})^{i}\gamma^{j}
when either
i>N
or
j>N
does not matter. And
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}(\gamma^{*})^{i}\gamma^{j}
vanishes for all
(i,j)\neq(k,l)
,
i\leq N,j\leq N
. Therefore,

\displaystyle(n!)\mathrm{coeff}_{u^{n}}F_{1,p}

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{n-k-l,k,l}\alpha_{1}^{k}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{l}|\vec{\alpha}|^{2(n-(k+l))}=

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{n-k-l,k,l}m(n-(k+l),k,l)

(4.2.8)

when
n\geq k+l
,
n-k\leq N
and
n-l\leq N
, and
(n!)\mathrm{coeff}_{u^{n}}F_{1,p}=0
otherwise. This implies the observability of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, since each member of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
can be written as
m(n-(k+l),k,l)
for some
(n,k,l)
that satisfies
2N\geq n\geq k+l
,
k\geq n-N
and
l\geq n-N
.
∎

Thm.
4.2.3
and its proof indicate that one can find a minimum number of distinct LO amplitudes that suffice to determine
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 3
.

There exist
\Gamma
of size
(N+1)^{2}
satisfying the conditions of Thm.
4.2.3
.

Proof.

\mathcal{P}_{N}
is a vector space of two-variable polynomials with dimension
(N+1)^{2}
. The condition on
\Gamma
- namely, that the linear map
p\in\mathcal{P}_{N}\mapsto(p(\gamma,\gamma^{*}))_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
is injective, is the same condition as the one required for the existence of a unique polynomial
p\in\mathcal{P}_{N}
for interpolating the data points
(\gamma,\gamma^{*},p(\gamma,\gamma^{*}))_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
. Picking the ordered polynomial basis

\displaystyle\{1,\gamma,\ldots,\gamma^{N},\gamma^{*},\gamma^{*}\gamma,\ldots,\gamma^{*}\gamma^{N},\ldots,(\gamma^{*})^{N},(\gamma^{*})^{N}\gamma,\ldots,(\gamma^{*})^{N}\gamma^{N}\},

(4.2.9)

the map
p\in\mathcal{P}_{N}\mapsto(p(\gamma,\gamma^{*}))_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
is represented by the matrix

\displaystyle J(N,\Gamma)=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc|cccc|c|cccc}1&\gamma_{1}&\ldots&\gamma_{1}^{N}&\gamma_{1}^{*}&\gamma_{1}\gamma_{1}^{*}&\ldots&\gamma_{1}^{N}\gamma_{1}^{*}&\ldots&(\gamma_{1}^{*})^{N}&\gamma_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{*})^{N}&\ldots&\gamma_{1}^{N}(\gamma_{1}^{*})^{N}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ldots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ldots&\vdots&\ldots&\vdots&\vdots&\ldots&\vdots\\
1&\gamma_{N_{p}}&\ldots&\gamma_{N_{p}}^{N}&\gamma_{N_{p}}^{*}&\gamma_{N_{p}}\gamma_{N_{p}}^{*}&\ldots&\gamma_{N_{p}}^{N}\gamma_{N_{p}}^{*}&\ldots&(\gamma_{N_{p}}^{*})^{N}&\gamma_{N_{p}}(\gamma_{N_{p}}^{*})^{N}&\ldots&\gamma_{N_{p}}^{N}(\gamma_{N_{p}}^{*})^{N}\end{array}\right),

where
N_{p}
is the size of
\Gamma
. Here,
J(N,\Gamma)
is acting on a vector of size
(N+1)^{2}
that is composed of the coefficients of
p
. The condition is then that this matrix be left-invertible so that the coefficients can be obtained from
(p(\gamma,\gamma^{*}))_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
. We now show that
M=(N+1)^{2}
mutually distinct points can be found such that the corresponding
J(N,\Gamma)
is invertible (in this case
J(N,\Gamma)
is a square matrix, so left-invertibility is equivalent to invertibility).

We can relate the complex matrix
J(N,\Gamma)
to the real matrix

\displaystyle R(N,\Gamma)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc|cccc|c|cccc}1&x_{1}&\ldots&x_{1}^{N}&y_{1}&x_{1}y_{1}&\ldots&x_{1}^{N}y_{1}&\ldots&y_{1}^{N}&x_{1}y_{1}^{N}&\ldots&x_{1}^{N}y_{1}^{N}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ldots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ldots&\vdots&\ldots&\vdots&\vdots&\ldots&\vdots\\
1&x_{N_{p}}&\ldots&x_{N_{p}}^{N}&y_{N_{p}}&x_{N_{p}}y_{N_{p}}&\ldots&x_{N_{p}}^{N}y_{N_{p}}&\ldots&y_{N_{p}}^{N}&x_{N_{p}}y_{N_{p}}^{N}&\ldots&x_{N_{p}}^{N}y_{N_{p}}^{N}\end{array}\right),

where
x_{i}=\textrm{Real}(\gamma_{i})
and
y_{i}=\textrm{Im}(\gamma_{i})
, by a right-multiplying
R(N,\Gamma)
with an invertible matrix. This is because the monomials in
\{\gamma^{n}(\gamma^{*})^{m}\}_{0\leq n\leq N,0\leq m\leq N}
can be expressed as linear combinations of the monomials in
\{x^{n}y^{m}\}_{0\leq n\leq N,0\leq m\leq N}
, and vice versa. The former is accomplished by writing any
\gamma^{n}(\gamma^{*})n
as
(x+iy)^{n}(x-iy)^{m}
and expanding, while the latter is accomplished by writing any
x^{n}y^{m}
as
(\frac{\gamma+\gamma^{*}}{2})^{n}(\frac{\gamma-\gamma^{*}}{2i})^{m}
and expanding. Thus, the invertibility of
R(N,\Gamma)
implies the invertibility of
J(N,\Gamma)
. The former is equivalent to the existence of a unique solution to a particular two-variable interpolation problem. More specifically, it is an instance of Lagrange interpolation, a discussion of which can be found in textbooks such as
[
27
]
. Cor. 5.6.3 in
[
27
]
states that Lagrange interpolation is almost regular, which in our case means that a unique solution exists for almost all sets of
(N+1)^{2}
points in the
2
-dimensional plane spanned by
x
and
y
.
∎

Notice that Prop.
3
does not show that
(N+1)^{2}
distinct probe amplitudes are necessary to determine
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. It only shows that the particular strategy of extracting
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
from
F_{1}
described in the proof of Thm.
4.2.3
requires at least
(N+1)^{2}
distinct probe amplitudes. We suspect that there exists a strategy to extract
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
from the measurement statistics using a fewer number of distinct probe amplitudes.

We have shown that given an input state with at most
N
photons, a finite number of probe amplitudes are sufficient to determine the expectations of the operators associated with the member of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. And the operators corresponding to the
m^{\prime}(a,b,c)
not in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
have zero expectations. We want to show that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
determines (and is determined by)
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
. Here is a stronger result.

Lemma 4.2.4
.

Assume
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
has at most
N
photons. Then, the set of expectations of the operators associated with the
m^{\prime}(a,b,c)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}
determines and is determined by
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
. Further, the expectations of the operators associated with the expressions in any strict subset of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}
are not sufficient to determine
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
, unless the subset chosen is
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}\setminus m^{\prime}(0,0,0)
.

Proof.

That the expectations of the operators associated with the
m^{\prime}(a,b,c)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}
are determined by
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is self-evident. To show the converse, we start with the same observations used in Cor.
4.2.2
. Namely, since
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
occupies modes
1
and
2
, the expectation of the operator corresponding to
m^{\prime}(i,j,k)
is equal to the expectation of
(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{k}a_{1}^{j}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{i}a_{2}^{i}
. Further, the anti-normally ordered monomials
a_{1}^{k}(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{j}a_{2}^{i}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{l}
have null expectations for
i\neq l
, and for
i=l
can be expressed as linear combinations of the normally-ordered monomials
(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{k}a_{1}^{j}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{i}a_{2}^{i}
. The latter fact, in our case, can be restated as follows. The expectation of any anti-normally ordered monomial of the form
a_{1}^{k}(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{j}a_{2}^{i}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{i}
can be written as a linear combination of the expectations of operators associated with the expressions in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

Using the correspondence of
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
with its Husimi function, the set of expectations of the monomials
a_{1}^{k}(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{j}a_{2}^{i}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{l}
for all tuples
(k,j,i,l)
correspond to the set of all moments of the Husimi function. As described in Sec.
2.6
, any state with bounded photon number has a determinate complex moment sequence. Thus, the set of expectations
a_{1}^{k}(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{j}a_{2}^{i}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{l}
for all tuples
(k,j,i,l)
determine
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
. According to the above observation, this means that the expectations of all operators associated with the
m^{\prime}(a,b,c)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
determine
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
. Next, we show that the operators associated with the
m^{\prime}(a,b,c)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
suffice.

Consider any
m^{\prime}(i,j,k)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}
. Then,
j>k
. The expectation of the corresponding operator is equal to

\displaystyle\langle(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{k}a_{1}^{j}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{i}a_{2}^{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\tr(\tilde\rho_2 (a_1^\dagger)^k a_1^j (a_2^\dagger)^i a_2^i )

\displaystyle=\left(\tr( \left(\tilde\rho_2 (a_1^\dagger)^k a_1^j (a_2^\dagger)^i a_2^i \right)^\dagger)\right)^{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\tr( (a_1^\dagger)^j a_1^k (a_2^\dagger)^i a_2^i \tilde\rho_2 )\right)^{*}

\displaystyle=\langle(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{j}a_{1}^{k}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{i}a_{2}^{i}\rangle^{*}.

(4.2.16)

Thus, for any
m^{\prime}(i,j,k)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}
, to obtain the expectation of the corresponding operator, one needs to take the complex conjugate of the expectation of the operator associated with
m^{\prime}(i,k,j)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}
.

Now we prove the final statement. The number of non-zero parameters in
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is equal to
(N+1)(N+2)(2N+3)/6
. The trace condition is given by the expectation of the operator corresponding to
m^{\prime}(0,0,0)
, and thus the latter does not contribute additional information about the state. Including the trace condition, the number of unknown parameters of
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is
(N+1)(N+2)(2N+3)/6-1
. The expectation of any operator associated with some
m^{\prime}(i,j,k)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}
is a linear combination of the non-zero parameters of
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
. Therefore, at least
(N+1)(N+2)(2N+3)/6-1
different
m^{\prime}(i,j,k)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}\setminus m^{\prime}(0,0,0)
are necessary to determine
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
. Counting the number of members of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}\setminus m^{\prime}(0,0,0)
one obtains
(N+1)(N+2)(2N+3)/6-1
. Therefore, every
m^{\prime}(i,j,k)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}\setminus m^{\prime}(0,0,0)
is necessary to determine
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
.
∎

Corollary 4.2.5
.

Let
\Gamma
satisfy the assumption in the statement of
Thm.
4.2.3
. Then
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
with at most

N
photons is determined by the measurement statistics of the counter at probe amplitudes in
\Gamma
.

Proof.

According to Thm.
4.2.3
, the measurement statistics at all
\gamma\in\Gamma
determine the expectations of the operators associated with the
m^{\prime}(a,b,c)\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. And the latter determine
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
according to Lem.
4.2.4
.
∎

Notice that the condition on the invertibility of the relationship between the measurement statistics at
\Gamma
and
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
assumes, at first glance, that the photon-number probabilities
p_{k}
are known for all
k
at each probe amplitude in
\Gamma
. This is because the proof of Cor.
4.2.5
assumes the operators
a_{1}^{k}(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{l}\vcentcolon\mathrel{\hat{n}_{2}^{c}}\vcentcolon
, where
c+\max(k,l)\leq N
, are observable, and that requires knowing the expectations of some of the powers of the total number operator at the output of the BS according to the transformation rule of the mode operators by the BS. And the powers of the total number operator are given as infinite sums of the projectors
\hat{D}_{k}
. However, since
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
has maximum photon number
N
, one can always find a finite set of photon number probabilities of the counter at various probe amplitudes in
\Gamma
that are sufficient to determine
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
. To see this, observe that the counter outcome probabilities at a particular
\gamma\in\Gamma
are given by the equation
p_{k}(\gamma)=\ tr_{\textsl{s}}(\Pi_{k}(\gamma)\tilde{\rho}_{2})
and are thus linear combinations of the non-zero elements of
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
. There is a finite number of the latter, and, since we know
\{\{p_{k}(\gamma)\}_{k=0}^{\infty}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
determines
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
by Cor.
4.2.5
, one is guaranteed to find a finite subset of
\{\{p_{k}(\gamma)\}_{k=0}^{\infty}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
that can be inverted to obtain the elements of
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
. Since the number of non-zero parameters in
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is equal to
(N+1)(N+2)(2N+3)/6
, the minimum size of this subset must be equal to
(N+1)(N+2)(2N+3)/6-1
, that is to the number of unknown parameters when the trace condition on
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is taken into account. This observation deserves its own statement.

Corollary 4.2.6
.

Let
\Gamma
satisfy the assumption in the statement of
Thm.
4.2.3
. Let
p_{k}(\gamma)
denote the probability of observing
k
photons at probe amplitude
\gamma
. Let
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
have maximum

N
photons. Then, there exist
(N+1)(N+2)(2N+3)/6-1
different probabilities in
\{\{p_{k}(\gamma)\}_{k=0}^{\infty}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
that determine
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
.

Proof.

See the discussion preceding the statement of the corollary.
∎

We leave it as an open problem to find specific sets of photon-number probabilities which determine
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
. A related open problem is to find an upper bound on
k
in the corollary statement for a given
\Gamma
.

The results derived so far assumed that both the magnitude and the phase of the probe can be adjusted. Now we consider what can be determined from the measurement statistics for a fixed probe magnitude.

Theorem 4.2.7
.

Assume the measurement statistics can be obtained for probe states with fixed magnitude
R
and with any phase
\theta
. Then,
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
can be determined from the counter outcome distribution iff the maximum photon number of
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is
N\leq 1
.

Proof.

As explained at the end of Sec.
4.1
, for a particular value of
\gamma
,
F(u,\gamma,\gamma^{*},\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}^{*})
, and by extension
F_{1}(u,\gamma,\gamma^{*},\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}^{*})
, generate a set of operators the expectations of which capture all of the information available by the measurement statistics. Thus, it suffices to show that the expectations of the set of observable coefficients of
F_{1}
associated with the powers of
u
for all values of
\theta
do not determine
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
when
N>1
, and determine it when
N=1
(the
N=0
case is trivial).

The coefficient of
u^{r}
is
(\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}}^{2}+\alpha_{1}\gamma^{*}+\alpha_{1}^{*}\gamma)^{r}/n!
. We substitute
\gamma=Re^{i\theta}
into the coefficients, expand the parenthesis, and collect the resulting terms by phase:

\displaystyle(n!)\textrm{coeff}_{u^{r}}F_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k+l=0}^{r}\binom{r}{k,l,r-k-l}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}}^{2(r-k-l)}\alpha_{1}^{k}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{l}(\gamma^{*})^{k}\gamma^{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k+l=0}^{r}\binom{r}{k,l,r-k-l}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}}^{2(r-k-l)}\alpha_{1}^{k}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{l}R^{k+l}e^{-i(k-l)\theta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=-r}^{r}e^{-i\theta t}\sum_{l=\max(0,-t)}^{\min(\lfloor\frac{r-t}{2}\rfloor,-t+\lfloor\frac{r+t}{2}\rfloor)}R^{2l+t}\binom{r}{l,l+t,r-t-2l}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{l}\alpha_{1}^{l+t}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}}^{2(r-t-2l)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=-r}^{r}e^{-i\theta t}\sum_{l=\max(0,-t)}^{\min(\lfloor\frac{r-t}{2}\rfloor,-t+\lfloor\frac{r+t}{2}\rfloor)}R^{2l+t}\binom{r}{l,l+t,r-t-2l}m(r-t-2l,l+t,l)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=-r}^{r}e^{-i\theta t}Q_{r,t}

(4.2.17)

where
Q_{r,t}
is the coefficient of
e^{-i\theta t}
. Given
the coefficient of
u^{r}
of
F_{1}
at
2r+1
different phases
\theta
, the
Q_{r,t}

can be extracted by interpolation, so the expectations of the operators
corresponding to the
Q_{r,t}
can be determined from the available
statistics. Conversely, the measurement statistics for any
\theta
can be determined from these expectations.

According to Lem.
4.2.4
it is necessary for every operator corresponding to every expression in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}
to be observable by the measurement statistics to determine
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
completely (the identity observable corresponding to the trace condition and associated with
m^{\prime}(0,0,0)
is always observable). Note that a derivation similar to Eq.
4.2.4
, but instead expressing
m(a,b,c)
as
(\absolutevalue{\vec{\beta}}^{2}+\absolutevalue{\alpha_1}^{2})^{a}\alpha_{1}^{b}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{c}
and expanding, shows that
\mathcal{M}
is in the span of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
(and that
\mathcal{M}_{>}
is in the span of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}
). We show below that
\mathcal{M}_{>}
, and by extension
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{>}
, cannot be determined from the set of
Q_{r,t}
when
N\geq 3
. In particular, notice that the parameter
t
is the difference in the powers of
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{1}^{*}
in
m(r-t-2l,l+t,l)
, and it is non-positive for all members of
\mathcal{M}_{>}
. Therefore, the different values of
t=0,-1,\ldots,-N
partition
\mathcal{M}_{>}
into mutually non-intersecting subsets. Notice also that
r
is the total degree of
m(r-t-2l,l+t,l)
, and thus can be used to partition each subset into smaller subsets. So the different tuples
(r,t)
partition
\mathcal{M}_{>}
into mutually non-intersecting subsets. Let us denote these subsets by
\mathcal{M}_{>,r,t}=\{m(a,b,c)\mid b=c+t,a+b+c=r\}
. Then,
Q_{r,t}
is a linear combination of the members of the subset
\mathcal{M}_{>,r,t}
. Therefore, it suffices to consider the tuple
(r,t)=(N,0)
and show that some of the expressions in
\mathcal{M}_{>,N,0}
cannot be determined from
Q_{N,0}
. Let us write
Q_{N,0}
explicitly:

\displaystyle Q_{N,0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}R^{2l}\binom{N}{l,l,N-2l}m(N-2l,l,l).

(4.2.18)

For
N\geq 2
,
Q_{N,0}
is a linear combination of more than one member of
\mathcal{M}_{>,N,0}
, and thus the operators associated with the expressions in
\mathcal{M}_{>,N,0}
cannot be individually observable.

For
N=1
, the set of
Q_{r,t}
’s with non-positive
t
, and excluding those
Q_{r,t}
corresponding to operators with zero expectations, is
\{Q_{0,0},Q_{1,-1},Q_{1,0},Q_{2,0}\}
. Each of these besides
Q_{2,0}
is proportional to one of the members of
\mathcal{M}_{>}\setminus m(0,1,1)=\{m(0,0,0),m(0,0,1),m(1,0,0)\}
, while
Q_{2,0}
is a linear combination of
m(0,1,1)
and
m(2,0,0)
. The latter corresponds to an operator with a zero expectation, and thus the expectation of the operator corresponding to
m(0,1,1)
can also be obtained.
∎

4.2.2
Results With Two Photon Counters

We now consider the WFH configuration with two photon counters. Thms.
4.2.1
,
4.2.3
, Prop.
3
, as well as Cors.
4.2.2
,
4.2.5
and
4.2.6
naturally apply to this configuration as well since the configuration with one photon counter is equivalent to forgetting the measurement outcomes of one of the photon counters in the two counter configuration. It might be possible to show that a lower bound on the size of
\Gamma
exists when both counters are utilized by a similar derivation that we did in the last section for the configuration with one counter. But we do not consider that problem. Using an additional counter increases the amount of information in the measurement statistics for a given probe amplitude. But for the sets of probe amplitudes assumed in Thm.
4.2.1
or Thm.
4.2.3
this information is already available in the measurement statistics of the single counter as
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is determined by the latter. We find that a situation where the second
photon counter helps is when the magnitude of the probe is fixed, in which case
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
can be determined with a two counter measurement configuration.

The generating function associated with the WFH configuration with both photon counters is
H_{1,\textrm{in}}(u,v,\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}^{*},\gamma,\gamma^{*})
defined in Eq.
4.1.5
. The observable operators associated with this configuration are given by the coefficients of the
u^{n}v^{m}
. Any linear combination of these operators is also observable. Performing a transformation of the variables
u
,
v
of
H_{1,\textrm{in}}
results in coefficients of the powers of the new variables which are linear combinations of the coefficients of the old variables. We use this fact repeatedly in the proof of the next theorem.

Theorem 4.2.8
.

Assume the Husimi function corresponding to
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
has a determinate complex moment sequence. Assume the measurement statistics can be obtained for probe states with fixed magnitude
R
and with any phase
\theta
. Then,
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is determined from the measurement statistics at all values of
\theta
.

Proposition 4
.

Denote the reduced state in the mode matching the probe, obtained from
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
, by
\rho_{r}
. Assume the Husimi function corresponding to
\rho_{r}
has a determinate complex moment sequence. The expectations of the operators associated with the expressions
(\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}}^{2}-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}}^{2})^{r}
at the outputs of the BS for non-negative integers
r
suffice to determine
\rho_{r}

Proof of Thm
4.2.8
and Prop.
4
.

We expand the exponent in the definition of
H_{1,\textrm{in}}
, taking advantage of
\vec{\gamma}=\gamma\vec{e}_{1}
:

\displaystyle H_{1,\textrm{in}}

\displaystyle=\exp(|\vec{\alpha}+\xi\vec{\gamma}|^{2}u + |\vec{\alpha}-\xi^{-1}\vec{\gamma}|^{2}v)

\displaystyle=\exp( ( \abs{\vec{\alpha}}^2 + \xi^2 \abs{\gamma}^2 + \xi\alpha_1 \gamma^* +\xi\alpha_1^* \gamma)u + (\abs{\vec{\alpha}}^2 + \xi^{-2} \abs{\gamma}^2 - \xi^{-1} \alpha_1 \gamma^* - \xi^{-1} \alpha_1^* \gamma) v)

\displaystyle=\exp( \abs{\vec{\alpha}}^2 (u+v) +\abs{\gamma}^2 ( \xi^2 u +\xi^{-2} v) + \alpha_1 \gamma^*(\xi u - \xi^{-1} v) + \alpha_1^* \gamma(\xi u - \xi^{-1} v ) )

(4.2.19)

Define the generating function
F_{2}(u,v,\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}^{*},\gamma,\gamma^{*})=H_{1,\textrm{in}}\exp(-\abs{\gamma}^2 ( \xi^2 u +\xi^{-2} v) )
. Then,

F_{2}=\exp( \abs{\vec{\alpha}}^2 (u+v) + \alpha_1 \gamma^*(\xi u - \xi^{-1} v) + \alpha_1^* \gamma(\xi u - \xi^{-1} v ) ).

(4.2.20)

F_{2}
is observable since it is a product of
H_{1,\textrm{in}}
with a scalar-valued (that is, not depending on
\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}^{*}
) generating function.

We treat
u
and
v
as real variables. Next, we define the independent variables
t=u+v
and
s=\xi u-\xi^{-1}v
, and express
\gamma
in polar form as
Re^{i\theta}
. Then,
F_{2}
is expressed as

F_{2}=\exp( \abs{\vec{\alpha}}^2 t + \alpha_1 R e^{- i\theta}s + \alpha_1^*R e^{i\theta} s )

(4.2.21)

Now, since
R
is fixed we can incorporate it into
s
and define the complex variable
z=se^{i\theta}
with its complex conjugate
z^{*}=se^{-i\theta}
to express
F_{2}
in the new variables as

F_{2}(t,z,z^{*},\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}^{*})=\exp( \abs{\vec{\alpha}}^2 t + \alpha_1 z^* + \alpha_1^* z)

(4.2.22)

The coefficient of
F_{2}
corresponding to the monomial
t^{a}(z^{*})^{b}z^{c}
can be seen to be proportional to
m(a,b,c)
. And thus the set of all
m(a,b,c)
are observable. It was shown in the proof of Cor.
4.2.2
that
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is determined by the
m(a,b,c)
if its Husimi function has a determinate complex moment sequence.

To prove the proposition statement, we first observe that it is sufficient to know the expectations of operators corresponding to the expressions
(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}
to determine the reduced state
\rho_{r}
. This is because the set of these expectations corresponds to the set of all moments of the Husimi function of
\rho_{r}
as described in Sec.
2.6
, and we have assumed that the Husimi function has a determinate moment sequence. Next, notice that Eq.
4.2.22
implies that these expectations can be obtained from
F_{2}(t,z,z^{*},\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}^{*})
at
t=0
- that is, from
F_{2}(0,z,z^{*},\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}^{*})=\exp( \alpha_1 z^* + \alpha_1^* z)
. Since
t=u+v
, the line
t=0
corresponds to
u=-v
. Thus, at the outputs of the BS we only require the part of the generating function
H=\exp(\abs{\vec{\alpha}}^2 u + \abs{\vec{\gamma}}^2 v)
(Eq.
4.1
) where
v=-u
. The coefficients of the powers of
u
in
\exp( (\abs{\vec{\alpha}}^2 - \abs{\vec{\gamma}}^2 )u)
are precisely the ones in the statement of the proposition.
∎

So far we have assumed that our detectors are photon number resolving, but in practice such detectors might not be available. Click detectors are generally significantly cheaper and more widely available. These are designed to give a binary answer upon measurement - either there were no photons registered (the detector does not click), or there were one or more photons registered (the detector clicks). This motivates us to investigate the conditions when the WFH configuration determines, or does not determine,
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
when the photon counters are substituted with click detectors that do not distinguish the modes. In the following a BS is “balanced” when the magnitudes of transmission and reflection coefficients are
1/\sqrt{2}
, and “unbalanced” otherwise.

Proposition 5
.

Assume the input state has at most
N
photons, and the measurement statistics are available for any probe amplitude. Then,
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
can be determined from the measurement statistics of the click detectors iff
N\leq 2
(
N\leq 1
) if the BS is unbalanced (balanced).

Proof.

The proof proceeds by considering explicitly the linear equations resulting from Born’s rule (Eq.
3.1
). Since
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
occupies only modes
1
and
2
, it suffices to restrict the projectors of the click detectors to the two-mode subspace. Let us denote the “no click” outcome with ’0’ and the “click” outcome with ’1’. Then, with 2 detectors there are 4 possible outcomes:
(0,0),(1,0),(0,1)
and
(1,1)
, where the first and second elements of each tuple denote the outcomes of counter 1 and counter 2 in Fig.
3.1
, treated as click detectors, respectively. The projectors corresponding to these outcomes, listed in the same order, are given by
\hat{D}_{00}
,
\hat{D}_{I0}=I_{1}\otimes\ket{0,0}\bra{0,0}-\hat{D}_{00}
,
\hat{D}_{0I}=\ket{0,0}\bra{0,0}\otimes I_{2}-\hat{D}_{00}
,
\hat{D}_{II}=I_{1}\otimes I_{2}-\hat{D}_{00}
, where
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are the identity operators on the output paths going to counter 1 and counter 2, respectively. Here we have ordered the state-spaces corresponding to the
4
modes in the tensor product according to the ordered mode basis
(a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2},b^{\prime}_{1},b^{\prime}_{2})
. Notice that
\hat{D}_{00}
and
\hat{D}_{II}
sum to the identity, and thus the information provided by the probability of outcome
(1,1)
is redundant. Further, to simplify our calculations, we can choose to work with the set of projectors
\hat{D}_{00}
,
\hat{D^{\prime}}_{I0}=\hat{D}_{I0}+\hat{D}_{00}=I_{1}\otimes\ket{0,0}\bra{0,0}
and
\hat{D^{\prime}}_{0I}=\hat{D}_{0I}+\hat{D}_{00}=\ket{0,0}\bra{0,0}\otimes I_{2}
instead of the mutually orthogonal set corresponding to the measurement outcomes, since these span the same space of operators.

For a given probe amplitude
\gamma
and BS unitary
U
, the operators on the input state corresponding to the projectors can be computed from the relation in Eq.
3.1.4
. In particular, the operator corresponding to
\hat{D}_{00}=\ket{0,0}\bra{0,0}\otimes\ket{0,0}\bra{0,0}
is computed to be

\displaystyle\Pi_{00}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\tr_{\textsl{p}}((I_{1}\otimes\ket{\gamma,0}\bra{\gamma,0})U^{\dagger}\hat{D}_{00}U)

\displaystyle=\tr_{\textsl{p}}((I_{1}\otimes\ket{\gamma,0}\bra{\gamma,0})\ket{0,0}\bra{0,0}\otimes\ket{0,0}\bra{0,0})

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\bra{0}\ket{\gamma}}^{2}\ket{0,0}\bra{0,0}

\displaystyle=e^{-\absolutevalue{\gamma}^{2}}\ket{0,0}\bra{0,0}.

(4.2.23)

The calculation of the operators on the input modes corresponding to
\hat{D^{\prime}}_{0I}
and
\hat{D^{\prime}}_{I0}
is slightly more involved. The BS unitary in mode space is
B=\begin{pmatrix}\eta&\zeta\\
\zeta&-\eta\end{pmatrix}\otimes\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
with our choice of ordering of the modes. We adopt the technique used in Eq.
2.4
in the calculation below.
\ket{\vec{0}}
stands for the vacuum on the full 4-mode space inside the partial trace, and for the 2-mode input space when the trace operation has been completed. Writing
\hat{D^{\prime}}_{I0}=\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\ket{k_1,k_2}\bra{k_1,k_2}\otimes\ket{0,0}\bra{0,0}
, and using the linearity of the partial trace, the corresponding operator on input modes is computed as

\displaystyle\Pi_{I0}(\gamma)=\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\tr_{\textsl{p}}((I_{1}\otimes\ket{\gamma,0}\bra{\gamma,0})U^{\dagger}\ket{k_1,k_2}\bra{k_1,k_2}\otimes\ket{0,0}\bra{0,0}U)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\tr_{\textsl{p}}((I_{1}\otimes\ket{\gamma,0}\bra{\gamma,0})\frac{1}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}U^{\dagger}(b_{1}^{\dagger})^{k_{1}}(b_{2}^{\dagger})^{k_{2}}\ket{\vec{0}}\bra{\vec{0}}(b_{1})^{k_{1}}(b_{2})^{k_{2}}U)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}\tr_{\textsl{p}}((I_{1}\otimes\ket{\gamma,0}\bra{\gamma,0})(\zeta a_{1}^{\dagger}-\eta b_{1}^{\dagger})^{k_{1}}(\zeta a_{2}^{\dagger}-\eta b_{2}^{\dagger})^{k_{2}}\ket{\vec{0}}\bra{\vec{0}}(\zeta a_{1}-\eta b_{1})^{k_{1}}(\zeta a_{2}-\eta b_{2})^{k_{2}})

\displaystyle=e^{-\absolutevalue{\gamma}^{2}}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}(\zeta a_{1}^{\dagger}-\eta\gamma^{*})^{k_{1}}(\zeta a_{2}^{\dagger})^{k_{2}}\ket{\vec{0}}\bra{\vec{0}}(\zeta a_{1}-\eta\gamma)^{k_{1}}(\zeta a_{2})^{k_{2}}

\displaystyle=e^{-\absolutevalue{\gamma}^{2}}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\frac{\zeta^{2k_{2}}}{k_{1}!}(\zeta a_{1}^{\dagger}-\eta\gamma^{*})^{k_{1}}\ket{0}\bra{0}(\zeta a_{1}-\eta\gamma)^{k_{1}}\otimes\ket{k_2}\bra{k_2}.

(4.2.24)

A similar calculation for the operator
\Pi_{0I}(\gamma)
corresponding to the projector
\hat{D^{\prime}}_{0I}
yields

\displaystyle\Pi_{0I}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=e^{-\absolutevalue{\gamma}^{2}}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\frac{\eta^{2k_{2}}}{k_{1}!}(\eta a_{1}^{\dagger}+\zeta\gamma^{*})^{k_{1}}\ket{0}\bra{0}(\eta a_{1}+\zeta\gamma)^{k_{1}}\otimes\ket{k_2}\bra{k_2}.

(4.2.25)

We use the representation of
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
in Cor.
3.3.4
, which for
K=1
has the form
\tilde{\rho}_{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{N}\chi_{n}\otimes\ket{n}\bra{n}
. The expectations of the operators are then linear combinations of the parameters of the matrices
\chi_{n}
. In particular,

\displaystyle\langle\Pi_{00}(\gamma)\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{-\absolutevalue{\gamma}^{2}}\bra{0,0}\tilde{\rho}_{2}\ket{0,0}

\displaystyle=e^{-\absolutevalue{\gamma}^{2}}\chi_{0,00},

(4.2.26)

so
\Pi_{00}(\gamma)
determines only the element
\chi_{0,00}
for any
\gamma
. We expand the expectation of
\Pi_{I0}(\gamma)
to obtain

\displaystyle\tr( \Pi_{I0}(\gamma) \tilde\rho_{2} )=e^{-\absolutevalue{\gamma}^{2}}\sum_{n}\tr( \chi_{n} \otimes\ket{n}\bra{n} \Pi_{I0}(\gamma) )

\displaystyle=e^{-\absolutevalue{\gamma}^{2}}\sum_{n}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\frac{\zeta^{2k_{2}}}{k_{1}!}\left[\bra{0}(\zeta a_{1}-\eta\gamma)^{k_{1}}\chi_{n}(\zeta a_{1}^{\dagger}-\eta\gamma^{*})^{k_{1}}\ket{0}\right]\absolutevalue{\bra{n}\ket{k_2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=e^{-\absolutevalue{\gamma}^{2}}\sum_{n}\sum_{k_{1}}\frac{\zeta^{2n}}{k_{1}!}\sum_{i=0}^{k_{1}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{1}}\binom{k_{1}}{i}\binom{k_{1}}{j}\zeta^{i+j}(-\eta\gamma^{*})^{k_{1}-i}(-\eta\gamma)^{k_{1}-j}\sqrt{i!j!}\bra{j}\chi_{n}\ket{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{N}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\zeta^{i+j}\sqrt{i!j!}\left[e^{-\absolutevalue{\gamma}^{2}}\sum_{k_{1}=max(i,j)}^{\infty}\frac{(-\eta)^{2k_{1}-i-j}}{k_{1}!}\binom{k_{1}}{i}\binom{k_{1}}{j}(\gamma^{*})^{k_{1}-i}\gamma^{k_{1}-j}\right](\sum_{n=0}^{N-\max(i,j)}\zeta^{2n}\chi_{n,ji}).

(4.2.27)

The expression is a linear combination of the terms
\sum_{n=0}^{N-\max(i,j)}\zeta^{2n}\chi_{n,ji}
, so at best it is possible to determine these terms from the expectations at different
\gamma
. A similar calculation for
\langle\Pi_{0I}(\gamma)\rangle
yields

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\eta^{i+j}\sqrt{i!j!}\left[e^{-\absolutevalue{\gamma}^{2}}\sum_{k_{1}=max(i,j)}^{\infty}\frac{\zeta^{2k_{1}-i-j}}{k_{1}!}\binom{k_{1}}{i}\binom{k_{1}}{j}(\gamma^{*})^{k_{1}-i}\gamma^{k_{1}-j}\right](\sum_{n=0}^{N-\max(i,j)}\eta^{2n}\chi_{n,ji})

(4.2.28)

This expression is similarly a linear combination of the terms
\sum_{n=0}^{N-\max(i,j)}\eta^{2n}\chi_{n,ji}
, so at best it is possible to determine these terms. If the BS is balanced,
\langle\Pi_{0I}(\gamma)\rangle
and
\langle\Pi_{I0}(\gamma)\rangle
are identical.

To see that for a BS balanced
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is not determined when
N>1
, it is sufficient to give an example of a
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
with
N=2
. In particular, assume that
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
is diagonal. Then there are
6
non-zero parameters, but the trace condition,
\langle\Pi_{00}(\gamma)\rangle
, as well as the terms
\sum_{n=0}^{N-\max(i,j)}\eta^{2n}\chi_{n,ji}
for the indices
(i,j)=(0,0),(1,1),(2,2)
, comprise
5
equations in these parameters. When the BS is unbalanced, we show that
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
cannot be determined when
N>2
by considering a diagonal
\tilde{\rho}
with at most
N=3
photons. The number of non-zero parameters is
10
. The trace condition and
\langle\Pi_{00}(\gamma)\rangle
supply one equation each. The terms
\sum_{n=0}^{N-\max(i,j)}\eta^{2n}\chi_{n,ji}
and
\sum_{n=0}^{N-\max(i,j)}\zeta^{2n}\chi_{n,ji}
supply
4
equations each for the indices
(i,j)=(0,0),(1,1),(2,2),(3,3)
, but notice that for the index
(i,j)=(3,3)
the equations are the same (both give
\chi_{0,33}
). Together, these comprise
9
equations for
10
unknowns. Using this style of investigation, it is straightforward to conclude that when
N=2
and the BS is unbalanced the unknown parameters of
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
can be obtained from the sums
\sum_{n=0}^{N-\max(i,j)}\eta^{2n}\chi_{n,ji}
and
\sum_{n=0}^{N-\max(i,j)}\zeta^{2n}\chi_{n,ji}
together with the trace condition and
\langle\Pi_{00}(\gamma)\rangle
. If the BS is balanced, the unknown parameters of
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
can be obtained when
N=1
. The requirement is that Eqs.
4.2.2
and
4.2.28
be invertible with respect to the terms
\sum_{n=0}^{N-\max(i,j)}\eta^{2n}\chi_{n,ji}
and
\sum_{n=0}^{N-\max(i,j)}\zeta^{2n}\chi_{n,ji}
. This is what we show next.

The argument we bring concerns Eq.
4.2.2
, and an identical argument can be made for Eq
4.2.28
. We observe that the terms in the square brackets in the expansions
4.2.2
are convergent power series for each tuple
(i,j)
. They converge for every value of
\gamma
since their linear combinations are equal to probability values, and thus their derivatives of any order in a neighborhood of
0
are well-defined. Notice that for the tuple
(i,j)
the monomials
\gamma^{k}(\gamma^{*})^{l}
in the corresponding power series all have a difference of
i-j
between the powers of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{*}
. For each
\delta=i-j\geq 0
, apply the operators
\partial^{i-j+k}_{\gamma}\partial^{k}_{\gamma^{*}}
for
k=0,\ldots,N-(i-j)
, and evaluate at
\gamma=0
, and for each
\delta=i-j<0
apply the operators
\partial^{k}_{\gamma}\partial^{k+j-i}_{\gamma^{*}}
for
k=0,\ldots,N-(j-i)
, and evaluate at
\gamma=0
. For each value of
\delta
one obtains
N-\absolutevalue{\delta}+1
equations involving the terms
\sum_{n=0}^{N-\max(i,j)}\zeta^{2n}\chi_{n,ji}
with
i-j=\delta
. There are
N-\absolutevalue{\delta}+1
such terms for each
\delta
, and the set of equations determines them. This is not hard to check by hand for
N\leq 2
.
∎

A natural extension of this measurement configuration with click detectors is to modify this configuration by putting a cascading array of BSs at each output of the original BS where the probe and the input state are interfered. Then, one would put click detectors at the output paths of every BS. If there are
k
click-detectors for each output of the original BS, this allows for distinguishing up to
k
photons in each output. Studying the symmetries and observables of such a configuration is beyond the scope of this work, however.

4.3
WFH Configurations With Arbitrary BS

In this section we identify a set of conditions under which, for a given WFH configuration, the available measurement statistics determine or do not determine
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
(
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
when
S_{1}=1
). These conditions depend on
\lambda(S)
, on the number of counters utilized, and on the set of probe amplitudes at which the measurement statistics are available. The generating function on the input state is given by
G_{\textrm{in}}(u,v,\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}^{*},\vec{\gamma},\vec{\gamma}^{*})
in Eq.
4.1.9
when both counters are used, and by
G_{\textrm{in},2}(u,\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}^{*},\vec{\gamma},\vec{\gamma}^{*})
in Eq.
4.1.10
when one of the counters is used. As discussed in the previous section, the expectations of the family of operators associated with
G_{\textrm{in}}
or
G_{\textrm{in},2}
(depending on the number of counters utilized) for each value of
\gamma
contain all the information in the measurement statistics about the input state for that
\gamma
. Therefore, if
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
(
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
when
S_{1}=1
) is not determined by these expectations, then it cannot be determined by the measurement statistics.

4.3.1
Results With Two Photon Counters

BS Characterized by a Partition With
S_{1}>1

Let us first determine the set of operators associated with
G_{\textrm{in}}
when
\gamma
is treated as a generating function variable - that is, it is assumed that the measurement statistics are available for all
\gamma
in a neighborhood of
0
. Recall that we introduced the notation
\vec{\alpha}=\oplus_{i=1}^{K}\vec{\alpha_{i}}
, where
\vec{\alpha_{i}}
is a vector of size
S_{i}
, and where the mode operators associated with the variables in
\vec{\alpha_{i}}
transform according to
B_{i}
. We currently assume
S_{1}>1
. Then we can write
\vec{\alpha}_{1}=\alpha_{1}\oplus\vec{\beta}
, where
\alpha_{1}
is the variable associated with
a_{1}
. We expand the first two terms in the exponent of
G_{\textrm{in}}
according to Eq.
4.1.9
:

\displaystyle G_{\textrm{in}}

\displaystyle=\exp( \abs{\eta_1 \alpha_1+ \zeta_1 \gamma}^2 u + \abs{\zeta_1 \alpha_1- \eta_1 \gamma}^2 v +( \eta_1^2 u + \zeta_1^2 v )\abs{\vec{\beta}}^2 + \sum_{i=2}^K (\eta_i^2 u + \zeta_i^2 v) \abs{ \vec{\alpha_i}}^2)

\displaystyle=\exp( \eta_1\zeta_1(\alpha_1 \gamma^* +\alpha_1^* \gamma)(u -v) + ( \zeta_1^2 u + \eta_1^2 v )\abs{\gamma}^2 + ( \eta_1^2 u + \zeta_1^2 v )\abs{\vec{\alpha_1}}^2 + \sum_{i=2}^K (\eta_i^2 u + \zeta_i^2 v) \abs{ \vec{\alpha_i}}^2).

(4.3.1)

We introduce the generating function
G_{1}=G_{\textrm{in}}\exp( -( \zeta_1^2 u + \eta_1^2 v )\abs{\gamma}^2)
. The coefficients of the momomials in the variables
u
,
v
,
\gamma
and
\gamma^{*}
of
G_{1}
are observable. The coefficient of the momomial
\gamma^{k}(\gamma^{*})^{l}
of
G_{1}
is a generating function in the variables
u
,
v
and, after multiplication with
k!l!
, is given by

\displaystyle G_{1,(k,l)}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\gamma}^{k}\partial_{\gamma^{*}}^{l}G_{1}\Bigg|_{\gamma=0}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\gamma}^{k}\partial_{\gamma^{*}}^{l}\exp( \eta_1\zeta_1(\alpha_1 \gamma^* +\alpha_1^* \gamma)(u -v) + ( \eta_1^2 u + \zeta_1^2 v )\abs{\vec{\alpha_1}}^2 + \sum_{i=2}^K (\eta_i^2 u + \zeta_i^2 v) \abs{ \vec{\alpha_i}}^2)\Bigg|_{\gamma=0}

\displaystyle=(\eta_{1}\eta_{2}(u-v))^{k+l}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}
—
_
γ
=0

\displaystyle=(\eta_{1}\zeta_{1}(u-v))^{k+l}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\exp( ( \eta_1^2 u + \zeta_1^2 v )\abs{\vec{\alpha_1}}^2 + \sum_{i=2}^K (\eta_i^2 u + \zeta_i^2 v) \abs{ \vec{\alpha_i}}^2).

(4.3.2)

For each
k
and
l
the coefficient of the monomial
\gamma^{k}(\gamma^{*})^{l}u^{a}v^{b}
of
G_{1}
corresponds to the coefficient of
u^{a}v^{b}
of the corresponding
G_{1,(k,l)}/(k!l!)
. However, the expressions for these coefficients are somewhat complicated, and we can simplify our task by considering the generating function
G^{\prime}_{1,(k,l)}=G_{1,(k,l)}/(\eta_{1}\zeta_{1}(u-v))^{k+l}
. Here, the division should be treated formally, so that

\displaystyle G^{\prime}_{1,(k,l)}=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\exp( ( \eta_1^2 u + \zeta_1^2 v )\abs{\vec{\alpha_1}}^2 + \sum_{i=2}^K (\eta_i^2 u + \zeta_i^2 v) \abs{ \vec{\alpha_i}}^2)

(4.3.3)

is defined everywhere.
G^{\prime}_{1,(k,l)}
is observable. To see this, consider the variable transformation of
u
and
v
into
u-v
and another expression independent of
u-v
(call it
t(u,v)
). The coefficients of
G_{1,(k,l)}
associated with these new variables are observable. Then, notice that the coefficient of
G^{\prime}_{1,(k,l)}
associated with the monomial
(u-v)^{n}t^{m}
is the same as the coefficient of
G_{1,(k,l)}
associated with the monomial
(u-v)^{n+k+l}t^{m}
. Let us denote the coefficients of
G^{\prime}_{1,(k,l)}
in the variables
u
and
v
by
g_{K}(k,l,a,b)/(a!b!)
:

\displaystyle g_{K}(k,l,a,b)=\left[\partial_{u}^{a}\partial_{v}^{b}G^{\prime}_{1,(k,l)}\right]_{u=0,v=0}

\displaystyle=\left[(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\eta_{i}^{2}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i}^{2})^{a}(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\zeta_{i}^{2}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i}^{2})^{b}\exp( \sum_{i=1}^K (\eta_i^2 u + \zeta_i^2 v) \abs{ \vec{\alpha_i}}^2)\right]_{u=0,v=0}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\eta_{i}^{2}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i}^{2})^{a}(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\zeta_{i}^{2}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i}^{2})^{b}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\left[\sum_{c_{1}+\ldots+c_{K}=a}\binom{a}{c_{1},\ldots,c_{K}}\prod_{i=1}^{K}\eta_{i}^{2c_{i}}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i}^{2c_{i}}\right]\left[\sum_{c_{1}+\ldots+c_{K}=b}\binom{a}{c_{1},\ldots,c_{K}}\prod_{i=1}^{K}\zeta_{i}^{2c_{i}}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i}^{2c_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\sum_{c_{1}+\ldots+c_{K}=a+b}C(c_{1},\ldots,c_{K})\prod_{i=1}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i}^{2c_{i}},

(4.3.4)

where
C(c_{1},\ldots,c_{K})
is an expression in terms of the powers of
\eta_{i}^{2}
and the
\zeta_{i}^{2}
that is determined by the product of the two multinomial expansions in the second to last line of Eq.
4.3.1
.

The discussion above implies that the measurement statistics at all
\gamma
in a neighborhood of
0
determine and are determined by the expectations of the operators corresponding to
g_{K}(k,l,a,b)
. We find that when
K
is large enough, one cannot determine
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
from these expectations. For this purpose we introduce the following sets of expressions in the variables
\vec{\alpha},\vec{\alpha}^{*}
:
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K}=\{m^{\prime}_{K}(k,l,\vec{c})=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\absolutevalue{\vec{\beta}}^{2c_{1}}\prod_{i=2}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i }^{2c_{i}}\mid k,l,c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
and
\mathcal{M}_{K}=\{m_{K}(k,l,\vec{c})=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\prod_{i=1}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i }^{2c_{i}}\mid k,l,c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
. The members of
\mathcal{M}_{K}
are linear combinations of the members of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K}
and vice versa. In particular, one can write any
m_{K}(k,l,\vec{c})\in\mathcal{M}_{K}
as

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\prod_{i=1}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i }^{2c_{i}}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\absolutevalue{\alpha_1 \oplus\vec{\beta} }^{2c_{1}}\prod_{i=2}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i }^{2c_{i}}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}(\absolutevalue{\alpha_1}^{2}+\absolutevalue{ \vec{\beta} }^{2})^{c_{1}}\prod_{i=2}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i }^{2c_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{c_{1}}\binom{c_{1}}{i}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k+i}\alpha_{1}^{l+i}\absolutevalue{ \vec{\beta} }^{2(c_{1}-i)}\prod_{i=2}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i }^{2c_{i}}.

(4.3.5)

And conversely,

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\absolutevalue{ \vec{\beta} }^{2c_{1}}\prod_{i=2}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i }^{2c_{i}}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}(\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha_1}}^{2}-\absolutevalue{\alpha_1}^{2})^{c_{1}}\prod_{2=1}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i }^{2c_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{c_{1}}\binom{c_{1}}{i}(-1)^{i}(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k+i}\alpha_{1}^{l+i}\absolutevalue{ \vec{\alpha}_1 }^{2(c_{1}-i)}\prod_{i=2}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i }^{2c_{i}}.

(4.3.6)

Let us further introduce the following sets of monomials:
\mathcal{M}_{K,\geq}=\{m_{K}(k,l,\vec{c})\mid k\geq l\}\subset\mathcal{M}_{K}
, and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}=\{m^{\prime}_{K}(k,l,\vec{c})\mid k\geq l\}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K}
. It can be seen from the Eqs.
4.3.1
and
4.3.1
that monomials in
\mathcal{M}_{K,\geq}
are linear combinations of monomials in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}
and vice versa. We also introduce the subsets
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K}(N)\subset\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K}
composed of the
m^{\prime}_{K}(k,l,\vec{c})
with
\max(k,l)+c_{1}+\ldots+c_{K}\leq N
, and the subsets
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}(N)\subset\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}
composed of the
m^{\prime}_{K}(k,l,\vec{c})
with
k+c_{1}+\ldots+c_{K}\leq N
for each
N
.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

Assume the Husimi function of
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
has a determinate complex moment sequence. Then, the set of expectations of all operators associated with the expressions in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}
(or, equivalently, in
\mathcal{M}_{K,\geq}
) determine and are determined by
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
. Further, the expectations of the operators associated with the expressions in any strict subset of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}
are not sufficient to determine
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
, unless the subset is
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}\setminus m_{K}^{\prime}(0,0,0)
.

Proof.

That
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
determines the expectation of every operator associated with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}
is evident. Further, if every
m^{\prime}_{K}(k,l,\vec{c})\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}
is observable then so is every
m^{\prime}_{K}(k,l,\vec{c})\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K}
. This is due to the fact that the expectation of the operator corresponding to any
m^{\prime}_{K}(k,l,\vec{c})\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K}\setminus\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}
is the complex conjugate of the expectation of the operator corresponding to
m^{\prime}_{K}(l,k,\vec{c})\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}
(natural extension of Eq.
4.2.1
to arbitrary
K
). Since
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
occupies the modes associated with the variables
\alpha_{1}
,
\beta_{1}
,
\alpha_{2,1},\ldots,\alpha_{K,1}
, the expectation of the operator corresponding to
(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\absolutevalue{\vec{\beta}}^{2c_{1}}\prod_{i=2}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i }^{2c_{i}}
is equal to the expectation of the operator corresponding to the monomial
(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\absolutevalue{\beta_1}^{2c_{1}}\prod_{i=2}^{K}\absolutevalue{\alpha_{i,1}}^{2c_{i}}
. Further, the monomials in the same variables as the
(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\absolutevalue{\beta_1}^{2c_{1}}\prod_{i=2}^{K}\absolutevalue{\alpha_{i,1}}^{2c_{i}}
, but not of the same form, are associated with operators the expectations of which vanish. This is because in every such monomial the power difference between at least one of the variables in
\{\beta,\alpha_{2,1},\ldots,\alpha_{K,1}\}
and its complex conjugate is not zero. The form of
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
then insures the vanishing of the expectations. Thus, the expectation of every normally-ordered monomial of mode operators in the modes occupied by
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
is assumed to be available. Therefore, the same applies to every anti-normally ordered monomial of mode operators in the modes occupied by
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
since these can be expressed as linear combinations of normally-ordered monomials of mode operators by repeated use of commutation relations. By the identification of the set of moments of the Husimi function and the set of expectations of anti-normally ordered monomials of mode operators, the complex moment sequence of the Husimi function is also available. According to the assumptions of the lemma statement, the latter is determinate, and, hence, the Husimi function is determined. Therefore,
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
is determined.

To prove the second part of the statement, assume some
m^{\prime}_{K}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime},\vec{c^{\prime}})\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}\setminus m_{K}^{\prime}(0,0,0)
is not observable. Then,
m^{\prime}_{K}(l^{\prime},k^{\prime},\vec{c^{\prime}})
is also not observable. Consider a
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
with at most
N=k^{\prime}+c^{\prime}_{1}+\ldots+c^{\prime}_{K}
photons. Then, the expectations of the operators associated with the
m^{\prime}_{K}(k,l,\vec{c})\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K}\setminus\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K}(N)
are zero. So, if the expectations associated with the observable operators in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K}(N)
do not determine
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
, then the latter cannot be determined. Since the expectation of every operator associated with
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K}(N)\setminus\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}(N))\setminus m^{\prime}_{K}(l^{\prime},k^{\prime},\vec{c^{\prime}})
is determined by the set of expectations of the operators associated with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}(N)\setminus m^{\prime}_{K}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime},\vec{c^{\prime}})
, if the latter do not determine
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
then
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
cannot be determined. Observe that the number of members of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}(N)
is equal to the number of elements of
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
in its upper triangular part. Further, the expectation of every operator associated with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,\geq}(N)
is a linear combination of these elements. Since one of these expectations is not available, and that expectation is not the trace condition,
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
cannot be determined.
∎

Theorem 4.3.2
.

Assume the BS is characterized by a partition with size
K
and
S_{1}>1
. Assume the measurement statistics are available for all
\gamma
in a neighborhood of
0
. Assume the Husimi function of
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
has a determinate complex moment sequence. Then,
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
can be determined from the measurement statistics of the counters iff
K\leq 2
.

Proof.

As explained above, the expectations of the operators associated with the
g(k,l,a,b)
contain all the information in the measurement statistics. We first prove that
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
cannot be determined when
K>2
by showing that not every monomial in
\mathcal{M}_{K,\geq}
can be obtained from the
g_{K}(k,l,a,b)
. At the risk of being redundant, we note that if
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
was characterized, then the expectation of every operator would be determined, and, therefore, if there exist operators associated with some
m_{K}(k,l,\vec{c})
the expectations of which cannot be ascertained, then
\tilde{\rho}_{K+1}
cannot be fully characterized.

It suffices to prove that not every operator corresponding to the expressions in
\mathcal{M}_{K,\geq}
is observable for
K=3
, since
\mathcal{M}_{3,\geq}\subset\mathcal{M}_{K,\geq}
for larger
K
. Eq.
4.3.1
can be written as:

\displaystyle g_{3}(k,l,a,b)=\sum_{c_{1}+c_{2}+c_{3}=a+b}C(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})m_{3}(k,l,c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}).

(4.3.7)

For a given pair
(k,l)
, and for a given non-negative integer
c
, the set of
m_{3}(k,l,c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})
with
c_{1}+c_{2}+c_{3}=c
appear only in the equations associated with the set of
g_{3}(k,l,a,b)
where
a+b=c
. Even for
c=1
there are
2
equations but
3
unknowns.

To prove that for
K\leq 2
the operators associated with the expressions in
\mathcal{M}_{K,\geq}
are observable, we consider
G^{\prime}_{1,(k,l)}
from Eq.
4.3.3
, which, for
K=2
, has the form

\displaystyle G^{\prime}_{1,(k,l)}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\exp( ( \eta_1^2 u + \zeta_1^2 v )\abs{\vec{\alpha_1}}^2 + (\eta_2^2 u + \zeta_2^2 v) \abs{ \vec{\alpha_2}}^2).

(4.3.8)

We perform the variable transformations
u^{\prime}=\eta_{1}^{2}u+\zeta_{1}^{2}v
and
v^{\prime}=\eta_{2}^{2}u+\zeta_{2}^{2}v
. Since
\eta_{1}\neq\eta_{2}
,
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
are independent variables. Then, notice that the coefficient of the monomial
(u^{\prime})^{c_{1}}(v^{\prime})^{c_{2}}
is equal to
m_{2}(k,l,c_{1},c_{2})/(c_{1}!c_{2}!)
. Thus, each member of
\mathcal{M}_{2,\geq}
is observable. Since
\mathcal{M}_{1,\geq}\subset\mathcal{M}_{2,\geq}
, the same is true for
K=1
as well.
∎

The measurement configurations discussed in Thm.
4.3.2
are particularly relevant for this thesis, as the experiment we describe in Chap.
6
is modeled by a BS characterized by a partition with
K=2
and
S_{1}\neq 1
. Therefore, it is important to consider this set of configurations in more detail and derive results similar to Thm.
4.2.3
and Prop.
3
. In particular, we assume that there are at most
N
photons in the input state so that the expectation of the operator associated with any
m_{2}(k,l,c_{1},c_{2})
where
\max(k,l)+c_{1}+c_{2}>N
vanishes. Let
\mathcal{P}_{N}
again denote the vector space spanned by the monomials
\gamma^{k}(\gamma^{*})^{l}
with
k\leq N
,
l\leq N
for
\gamma\in\mathbb{C}
. Further, let
\mathcal{M}_{2}(N)
denote the subset of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
composed of the
m_{2}(k,l,c_{1},c_{2})
with
\max(k,l)+c_{1}+c_{2}\leq N
, and we have already defined
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{2}(N)
to be the subset of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{2}
composed of the
m^{\prime}_{2}(k,l,c_{1},c_{2})
with
\max(k,l)+c_{1}+c_{2}\leq N
. According to Eqs.
4.3.1
and
4.3.1

\mathcal{M}_{2}(N)
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{2}(N)
can be expressed in terms of each other. Since the expectations of the operators associated with the
m_{2}(k,l,c_{1},c_{2})
where
\max(k,l)+c_{1}+c_{2}>N
vanish, according to Lem.
4.3.1
,
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
is determined by the expectations of the operators associated with the expressions in
\mathcal{M}_{2}(N)
(equivalently, in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{2}(N)
). We show that a finite set of probe amplitudes is sufficient to determine the latter.

Theorem 4.3.3
.

Assume the BS is characterized by a partition with size
2
and
S_{1}>1
. Assume
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
has at most
N
photons. Let a finite set
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{C}
satisfy the following
condition: For all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{N}
such that
p(\Gamma)=\{0\}
, we
have
p=0
. Then, given the outcome distributions of both counters at all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, the expectations of the operators associated with the members of
\mathcal{M}_{2}(N)
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{2}(N)
can be determined. Therefore,
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
is determined by the measurement statistics.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the statement for
\mathcal{M}_{2}(N)
. The generating function
G_{1}
is given by

\displaystyle G_{1}(u,v,\gamma,\gamma^{*})=\exp( \eta_1\zeta_1(\alpha_1 \gamma^* +\alpha_1^* \gamma)(u -v) + ( \eta_1^2 u + \zeta_1^2 v )\abs{\vec{\alpha_1}}^2 + (\eta_2^2 u + \zeta_2^2 v) \abs{ \vec{\alpha_2}}^2).

(4.3.9)

The assumption on
\Gamma
in the theorem statement implies that for any given monomial
p=\gamma^{l}(\gamma^{*})^{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{N}
there exist coefficients
(c_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
such that for all
monomials
q\in\mathcal{P}_{N}
different from
p
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}q(\gamma,\gamma^{*})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}p(\gamma,\gamma^{*})

\displaystyle=1.

(4.3.10)

We consider the sum
G_{1,p}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}G_{1}(u,v,\gamma,\gamma^{*})
. We expand
G_{1}
in terms of the
G_{1,(i,j)}
in Eq.
4.3.1
to obtain

\displaystyle G_{1,p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!j!}G_{1,(i,j)}(\gamma^{*})^{i}\gamma^{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!j!}G_{1,(i,j)}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}(\gamma^{*})^{i}\gamma^{j}.

(4.3.11)

For
i\leq N
,
j\leq N
and
(i,j)\neq(k,l)
the sum
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}(\gamma^{*})^{i}\gamma^{j}
vanishes. Furthermore, since
G_{1,(i,j)}
is proportional to a product of a power series with the monomial
(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{i}\alpha_{1}^{j}
, when
i>N
or
j>N
every coefficient of
G_{1,(i,j)}
is associated with an operator with zero expectation. Therefore, taking the expectations of the operator-valued generating functions associated with both sides of Eq.
4.3.1
, the expectation of the operator-valued generating function associated with
G_{1,(k,l)}
can be determined. Since, according to the proof of Thm.
4.3.2
, the
m_{2}(k,l,c_{1},c_{2})
are in the span of the coefficients of
G_{1,(k,l)}
, and
G_{1,(k,l)}
is observable for every
k\leq N
,
l\leq N
, every member of
\mathcal{M}_{2}(N)
is observable.
∎

Note that since the polynomial space is
(N+1)^{2}
-dimensional, there exist
\Gamma
of size
(N+1)^{2}
that satisfy the conditions of Thm.
4.3.3
. The proof is given in Prop.
3
. Thus,
(N+1)^{2}
different probe amplitudes are sufficient to determine
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
for a BS characterized by a partition with size
K=2
and
S_{1}>1
. Further, a result similar to Cor.
4.2.6
can be derived for
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
with at most
N
photons. The number of non-zero parameters of
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
is equal to
\frac{(N+1)(N+2)^{2}(N+3)}{12}
.

Corollary 4.3.4
.

Let
\Gamma
satisfy the assumption in the statement of
Thm.
4.3.3
. Let
p_{kl}(\gamma)
denote the probability of observing
k
and
l
photons by the counter 1 and counter 2, respectively, at the probe amplitude
\gamma
. Let
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
have at most
N
photons. Then, there exist
\frac{(N+1)(N+2)^{2}(N+3)}{12}-1
different probabilities in
\{\{p_{kl}(\gamma)\}_{k,l=0}^{\infty}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
that determine
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
.

Proof.

The number of unknown parameters in
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
is equal to
u(N)=\frac{(N+1)(N+2)^{2}(N+3)}{12}-1
, when the trace condition is taken into account. According to Thm
4.3.3
the measurement statistics at all
\gamma\in\Gamma
determine
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
. The measurement statistics are encoded in the set of probabilities
p_{kl}(\gamma)
, which are linear combinations of the non-zero parameters of
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
according to Eq.
3.1
. Since the number of unknown parameters is
u(N)
, one can find a finite subset of the
p_{kl}(\gamma)
of size
u(N)
that determines
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
.
∎

We leave it as an open problem to find specific finite sets of probabilities of the outcomes of the counters and specific sets
\Gamma
for a given
N
that determine
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
. Several similar problems can also be posed. For example, given a
\Gamma
that satisfies the conditions of Thm.
4.3.3
, find an upper bound on the outcomes
k
and
l
such that there exists a set of probabilities, with outcomes less than the corresponding upper bounds, that determines
\tilde{\rho}_{3}
.

BS Characterized by a Partition With
S_{1}=1

So far we have considered measurement configurations that have a BS characterized by a partition where
S_{1}>1
. Let us now consider the BSs characterized by partitions where
S_{1}=1
. In this case
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
occupies
K
modes - in particular,
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}=\sum_{i_{2}}\ldots\sum_{i_{K}}\chi_{\vec{i}}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{k=2}^{K}\ket{i_k}\bra{i_k}\right)
, where for a particular
k=2,\ldots,S
the
\ket{i_k}
are Fock states in the mode
a_{S_{1}+\ldots+S_{K-1}+1}
. We are guided by the same overall strategy we utilized for
S_{1}>1
. Let us introduce the following sets of monomials in the variables
\vec{\alpha}
and
\vec{\alpha}^{*}
:
\mathcal{M}_{K,1}=\{m_{K,1}(k,l,c_{2},\ldots,c_{K})=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\prod_{i=2}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i }^{2c_{i}}\mid k,l,c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
and
\mathcal{M}_{K,1,\geq}=\{m_{K,1}(k,l,c_{2},\ldots,c_{K})\mid k\geq l\}\subset\mathcal{M}_{K,1}
. We further define the subsets
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1}(N)\subset\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1}
that are composed of the
m^{\prime}_{K,1}(k,l,c_{2},\ldots,c_{K})
with
\max(k,l)+c_{2}+\ldots+c_{K}\leq N
, and the subsets
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1,\geq}(N)\subset\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1,\geq}
composed of the
m^{\prime}_{K,1}(k,l,c_{2},\ldots,c_{K})
with
k+c_{2}+\ldots+c_{K}\leq N
for each
N
.

Lemma 4.3.5
.

Assume the Husimi function of
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
has a determinate complex moment sequence. Then, the set of expectations of all operators associated with the expressions in
\mathcal{M}_{K,1,\geq}
determine and are determined by
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
. Further, the expectations of the operators associated with the expressions in any strict subset of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1,\geq}
are not sufficient to determine
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
, unless the subset is
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1,\geq}\setminus m_{K,1}^{\prime}(0,0,0,\ldots,0)
.

Proof.

The lemma can be proved using the same strategy as in the proof of Lem.
4.3.1
. We outline the steps without mentioning all the details which can be inferred from the proof of Lem.
4.3.1
. The proof of the first statement is built around showing that, if every member of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1,\geq}
is observable, the expectation of every anti-normally ordered monomial of mode operators in the modes occupied by
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
is available. Then, since these expectations correspond to the moments of the Husimi function, and the latter has a determinate moment sequence, it is determined by this set of expectations. Since any anti-normally ordered monomial of mode operators is a linear combination of normally-ordered monomials of mode operators, this set of expectations is determined by the set of expectations of all normally-ordered monomials of mode operators in the modes occupied by
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
. The expressions associated with the latter have the form
(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\prod_{i=2}^{K}\alpha_{i,1}^{c_{i}}(\alpha^{*}_{i,1})^{c^{\prime}_{i}}
. For the expressions where
c_{i}\neq c_{i}^{\prime}
for at least one
i
, the expectations of the corresponding operators vanish due to the form of
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
, while for any expression where
c_{i}=c_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
, the expectation of the corresponding operator is equal to the expectation associated with
m_{K,1}(k,l,c_{2},\ldots,c_{K})
. And the expectation associated with any
m_{K,1}(k,l,c_{2},\ldots,c_{K})\notin\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1,\geq}
is determined by the expectation associated with
m_{K,1}(l,k,c_{2},\ldots,c_{K})\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1,\geq}
.

The proof of the second statement is built around showing that, if some

m_{K,1}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime},c^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,c^{\prime}_{K})\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1,\geq}\setminus m_{K,1}^{\prime}(0,0,0,\ldots,0)

is not observable, then an arbitrary
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
with at most
N=k^{\prime}+c_{2}^{\prime}+\ldots+c_{K}^{\prime}
photons cannot be determined. The reason is that the expectations of the operators associated with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1,\geq}(N)
are linear combinations of the elements in the upper triangular part of
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
, and the number of expectations equals the number of elements. Thus the upper triangular part of
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
cannot be determined from the subset of the expectations associated with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1,\geq}(N)\setminus m_{K,1}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime},c^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,c^{\prime}_{K})
. And the expectations of all operators associated with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1,\geq}\setminus\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{K,1,\geq}(N)
are zero, and therefore, do not add any information about
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
.
∎

Notice that for the case
S_{1}=1
the second line of Eq.
4.3.1
is still valid (with
\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}}^{2}=\absolutevalue{\alpha_1}^{2}
). Similarly, Eqs.
4.3.1
,
4.3.1
and
4.3.1
are also valid as their derivations do not assume a restriction on the dimension of
\vec{\alpha}_{1}
. Remember that the
g_{K}(k,l,a,b)
in Eq.
4.3.1
are observable when the measurement statistics are available for all
\gamma
in a neighborhood of
0
. Moreover, the expectations of the operators corresponding to the
g_{K}(k,l,a,b)
contain all the information in the measurement statistics. In this case
g_{K}(k,l,a,b)
becomes

\displaystyle g_{K}(k,l,a,b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c_{1}+\ldots+c_{K}=a+b}C(c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{K})(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k+c_{1}}\alpha_{1}^{l+c_{1}}\prod_{i=2}^{K}\absolutevalue{\vec{\alpha}_i}^{2c_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{c_{1}+\ldots+c_{K}=a+b}C(c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{K})m_{K,1}(k+c_{1},l+c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{K}).

(4.3.12)

Theorem 4.3.6
.

Assume the BS is characterized by a partition with size
K
and
S_{1}=1
. Assume the measurement statistics are available for all
\gamma
in a neighborhood of
0
. Assume the Husimi function of
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
has a determinate complex moment sequence. Then,
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
can be determined from the measurement statistics of the counters iff
K\leq 3
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds in the same manner as the proof of Thm.
4.3.2
. We first prove that
\tilde{\rho}_{1,K}
cannot be determined when
K>3
by showing that there exist members of
\mathcal{M}_{K,1,\geq}
which cannot be written as linear combinations of the
g_{K}(k,l,a,b)
. It suffices to show this for
K=4
, in which case

\displaystyle g_{4}(k,l,a,b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c_{1}+c_{2}+c_{3}+c_{4}=a+b}C(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4})m_{4,1}(k+c_{1},l+c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}).

(4.3.13)

Consider the subset
\mathcal{M}_{4,1,\geq}^{0}=\{m_{4,1}(k,k,c_{2},c_{3},c_{4})\mid k\geq 0,c_{i}\geq 0\textrm{ for all }i\}\subset\mathcal{M}_{4,1,\geq}
.
\mathcal{M}_{4,1,\geq}^{0}
only affects the
g_{4}(k,l,a,b)
where
k=l
. Conversely,
g_{4}(k,k,a,b)
only depends on the members of
\mathcal{M}_{4,1,\geq}^{0}
. Consider the set of
g_{4}(k,k,a,b)
with
k+a+b=1
. There are
3
members in this set. The
g_{4}(k,k,a,b)
in this set only depend on the
m_{4,1}(k,k,c_{2},c_{3},c_{4})\in\mathcal{M}_{4,1,\geq}^{0}
with
k+c_{2}+c_{3}+c_{4}=1
, and there are
4
such
m_{4,1}(k,k,c_{2},c_{3},c_{4})
. Conversely, the set of
m_{4,1}(k,k,c_{2},c_{3},c_{4})
with
k+c_{2}+c_{3}+c_{4}=1
only affects the
g_{4}(k,k,a,b)
with
k+a+b=1
. Thus, the former cannot be expressed in terms of the latter.

To show that for
K\leq 3
every expression in
\mathcal{M}_{K,1,\geq}
can be expressed as a linear combination of the
g_{K}(k,l,a,b)
, it suffices to show this for
K=3
. Then, starting with
G^{\prime}_{1,(k,l)}
in Eq.
4.3.3
,

\displaystyle G^{\prime}_{1,(k,l)}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\exp( ( \eta_1^2 u + \zeta_1^2 v )\abs{\alpha_1}^2 + (\eta_2^2 u + \zeta_2^2 v) \abs{ \vec{\alpha_2}}^2+ (\eta_3^2 u + \zeta_3^2 v) \abs{ \vec{\alpha_3}}^2).

(4.3.14)

Since
\eta_{2}\neq\eta_{3}
,
\eta_{2}^{2}u+\zeta_{2}^{2}v
and
\eta_{3}^{2}u+\zeta_{3}^{2}v
are linearly independent. So we can introduce the variable transformation
u^{\prime}=\eta_{2}^{2}u+\zeta_{2}^{2}v
and
v^{\prime}=\eta_{3}^{2}u+\zeta_{3}^{2}v
. Then, the expression
\eta_{1}^{2}u+\zeta_{1}^{2}v
in the exponent is a linear function of
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
:
\eta_{1}^{2}u+\zeta_{1}^{2}v=h_{1}u^{\prime}+h_{2}v^{\prime}
for some
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
. The coefficient of the monomial
(u^{\prime})^{a}(v^{\prime})^{b}
, after multiplying by
a!b!
, is given by

\displaystyle\left[\partial_{u^{\prime}}^{a}\partial_{v^{\prime}}^{b}G^{\prime}_{1,(k,l)}\right]_{u^{\prime}=0,v^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\left[\partial_{u^{\prime}}^{a}\partial_{v^{\prime}}^{b}\exp( (h_1 u' + h_2 v' ) \abs{\alpha_1}^2 + u' \abs{ \vec{\alpha_2}}^2+ v' \abs{ \vec{\alpha_3}}^2)\right]_{u^{\prime}=0,v^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}\left[\partial_{u^{\prime}}^{a}\partial_{v^{\prime}}^{b}\exp( u'(h_1 \abs{\alpha_1}^2 + \abs{ \vec{\alpha_2}}^2 ) +v'(h_2 \abs{\alpha_1}^2 + \abs{ \vec{\alpha_3}}^2) )\right]_{u^{\prime}=0,v^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{*})^{k}\alpha_{1}^{l}(h_{1}\absolutevalue{\alpha_1}^{2}+\absolutevalue{ \vec{\alpha_2}}^{2})^{a}(h_{2}\absolutevalue{\alpha_1}^{2}+\absolutevalue{ \vec{\alpha_3}}^{2})^{b}.

(4.3.15)

Let us denote these terms by
g^{\prime}_{3}(k,l,a,b)
. The observability of every member of
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}
can be shown by recursion. Because the recursive sequence is somewhat complicated, we build it gradually for the reader by considering consecutively larger subsets of
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}
. For each
d\geq 0
let us introduce the set of sets
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d}=\{\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d,a,b}=\{m_{3,1}(k+d,k,a,b)\mid k\geq 0\}\}_{a,b=0}^{\infty}
. The
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d}
do not intersect and their union is
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}
. The same is true for the
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d,a,b}
. We show that, for a given
d
, the corresponding
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d}
is determined by
\{g^{\prime}_{3}(k+d,k,a,b)\}_{a,b,k=0^{\infty}}
. In the following, by “one can obtain the expression” we mean the expression can be written as a linear combination of observable expressions. We start with
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d,0,0}
. It can be seen that
m_{3,1}(k+d,k,0,0)
is equal to
g^{\prime}_{3}(k+d,d,0,0)
and thus
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d,0,0}
is observable. Next, consider the
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d,a,0}
for
a\geq 1
. Any
m_{3,1}(k^{\prime}+d,k^{\prime},a^{\prime},0)
can be written as a linear combination of
g^{\prime}_{3}(k^{\prime}+d,d,a^{\prime},0)
and the
m_{3,1}(k+d,k,a,0)
with
(a,k)=(a^{\prime}-1,k^{\prime}+1),(a^{\prime}-2,k^{\prime}+2)\ldots,(0,k^{\prime}+a^{\prime})
by rearranging the terms across the equality sign in the expansion of
g^{\prime}_{3}(k^{\prime}+d,d,a^{\prime},0)
. Thus, starting with
a^{\prime}=1
, one can obtain any
m_{3,1}(k+d,k,a,0)
by recursion. The same argument shows that every member of
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d,0,b}
for
b\geq 1
is a linear combination of the
g^{\prime}_{3}(k+d,d,0,b)
.

We generalize this recursive approach of obtaining the members of
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d}
to any tuple
(a,b)
as follows. We have already shown that
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d,a,0}
and
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d,0,b}
are observable sets for any
a
and
b
, respectively. We start by considering the
\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d,a,1}
for all
a
. Without going too much into the details, any
m_{3,1}(k^{\prime}+d,k^{\prime},a^{\prime},1)
can be written as a linear combination of
g^{\prime}_{3}(k^{\prime}+d,d,a^{\prime},1)
, some of the members belonging to
\{\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d,a,0}\}_{a}
, and the
m_{3,1}(k+d,k,a,1)
with
(a,k)=(a^{\prime}-1,k^{\prime}+1),(a^{\prime}-2,k^{\prime}+2)\ldots,(0,k^{\prime}+a^{\prime})
by rearranging the terms across the equality sign in the expansion of
g^{\prime}_{3}(k^{\prime}+d,d,a^{\prime},1)
. Thus, starting with
a^{\prime}=0
, every
m_{3,1}(k^{\prime}+d,k^{\prime},a^{\prime},1)
can be obtained by recursion. Finally, for an arbitrary
b
,
m_{3,1}(k^{\prime}+d,k^{\prime},a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
can be written as a linear combination of
g^{\prime}_{3}(k^{\prime}+d,d,a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
, some of the members belonging to
\{\{\mathcal{M}_{3,1,\geq}^{d,a,b}\}_{a=0}^{\infty}\}_{b=0}^{b^{\prime}-1}
, and the
m_{3,1}(k+d,k,a,b^{\prime})
with
(a,k)=(a^{\prime}-1,k^{\prime}+1),(a^{\prime}-2,k^{\prime}+2)\ldots,(0,k^{\prime}+a^{\prime})
by rearranging the terms across the equality sign in the expansion of
g^{\prime}_{3}(k^{\prime}+d,d,a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
. Thus, starting with
b^{\prime}=1
, the
m_{3,1}(k^{\prime}+d,k^{\prime},a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
can be obtained by recursion.
∎

4.3.2
Results With One Photon Counter

When one counter is used, the generating function corresponding to the WFH configuration is
G_{\textrm{in},2}
in Eq.
4.1.10
. We first consider BSs characterized by partitions where
S_{1}>1
